## Your practical task

The codebase has already been scanned. Your job is to find and understand the single highest-priority thing to work on:

4. Run `python -m desloppify --lang python next --explain` to see if the explanation helps
5. Try running `python -m desloppify --lang python show` with whatever the finding's detector or file is, to get more context

## Your meta-reflection task

After completing the practical task above, write a detailed reflection covering:

1. **Priority understanding**: Did you understand WHY this item was the top priority? Was the tier/confidence system clear? Rate 1-10.

2. **Impact clarity**: Did the tool tell you how much fixing this would improve your score? Was there a "fixing this is worth ~X pts" line? Was it helpful? Rate 1-10.

3. **Actionability**: After reading the `next` output, did you know exactly what to do? Could you have fixed the issue and resolved it? Rate 1-10.

4. **Subjective items**: If you encountered a subjective dimension item, did the tool warn you that re-review can lower scores? Did you understand the distinction between mechanical and subjective?

5. **Score context in next**: Did the `next` output show you your current scores? Did it explain what the scores mean, or did you need that context from elsewhere?

6. **Resolution flow**: Was it clear how to mark something as fixed or wontfix after you fix it? Did the tool show you the exact commands?

7. **Confusing moments**: List every moment where you were confused or had to re-read. Quote exact text.

8. **Overall UX rating**: Rate the `next` command output 1-10 for a first-time agent user. What single change would improve it most?

You are a developer agent who has NEVER used `desloppify` before. You've been told it's a "codebase health tool" and your job is to maximize the score as efficiently as possible.

## Your practical task

The codebase has already been scanned. Your job is to figure out the optimal strategy for score improvement:

1. Run `python -m desloppify --lang python status` to see current scores
2. Run `python -m desloppify --lang python plan` to see the full breakdown
3. Run `python -m desloppify --lang python next --count 5` to see the top 5 items
4. Try to figure out: Which of the 4 scores should I be maximizing? Is that clearly communicated?
5. Try to figure out: What's the fastest path to improve the strict score? Are there auto-fixable items?
6. Run `python -m desloppify --lang python next --tier 1` and `next --tier 2` to compare high vs medium priority items
7. Try to understand: If I wontfix a bunch of low-priority items, what happens to my scores? Is the tool honest about the tradeoffs?
8. Try to figure out: Are there any items that would have outsized impact on the score? Does the tool tell me about score impact per fix?

DO NOT read the source code to understand what things mean. Act like a real user who only has terminal output.

## Your meta-reflection task

After completing the practical task above, write a detailed reflection covering:

1. **Goal clarity**: Did you quickly understand which score to maximize? Was "strict is your north star" communicated? Rate 1-10.

2. **Strategy formation**: Were you able to form an efficient improvement strategy from the tool's output? What information helped most? Rate 1-10.

3. **Impact visibility**: Did the tool show you score impact estimates for individual fixes? Was it useful for prioritization? Rate 1-10.

4. **Wontfix honesty**: Did the tool clearly communicate the cost of wontfix? Did you see warnings about overall↔strict gap or "hidden debt"? Rate 1-10 for transparency.

5. **Auto-fix discovery**: Was it clear which items have auto-fixers available? Did you see "[auto]" tags or auto-fix commands? Rate 1-10.

6. **Subjective vs mechanical**: Did you understand that some dimensions require human review while others are code-fixable? Was this distinction clear? Rate 1-10.

7. **Score model transparency**: Do you now understand how the overall score is computed (40% mechanical + 60% subjective)? Where did you learn this? Rate 1-10.

8. **Confusing moments**: List every moment where you were confused, misled, or had to guess. Quote exact text.

9. **Gaming resistance**: Did the tool seem to discourage gaming (wontfixing everything, inflating scores)? What mechanisms did you notice?

10. **Overall UX rating**: Rate the tool 1-10 for a score-maximizing agent. What single change would improve it most?

Format your meta-reflection as a structured report with headers.

  TypedDict-backed payload/state boundaries are softened by `.get()` + default coercion, masking schema drift and invalid runtime shapes.
  Type: Design review (requires judgment)
  File: .
  ID:   review::.::holistic::abstraction_fitness::typed_dict_contract_softening::5e16e1c8

  Suggestion: Introduce strict boundary constructors for `ReviewImportPayload` and `StateModel` slices: validate required keys explicitly, fail with structured errors on invalid shape, and reserve default insertion for truly optional fields only.

  [2/5]
  (Tier 1, high confidence)
  ────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────
  flat_dirs returns a total count that does not match the directory set it evaluates
  Type: Design review (requires judgment)
  File: .
  ID:   review::.::holistic::contract_coherence::flat_dirs_denominator_mismatch::433d59b8

  Suggestion: Return len(all_dirs) (or another explicitly named analyzed_count) and update call sites/labels so the second tuple value consistently represents analyzed directories.

  [3/5]
  (Tier 1, high confidence)
  ────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────
  single_use detector contract claims symbol-level/export checks but runs only file-level importer counting
  Type: Design review (requires judgment)
  File: .
  ID:   review::.::holistic::contract_coherence::single_use_contract_overstates_behavior::104f7df5

  Suggestion: Align interface and behavior: either implement symbol/export-level analysis (with symbol-aware graph input) or rename/re-document the detector and finding text to explicitly describe file-level single-import heuristics.

  [4/5]
  (Tier 1, high confidence)
  ────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────
  Visualization flow swallows broad exceptions during language/plugin resolution, masking real failures behind fallback behavior.
  Type: Design review (requires judgment)
  File: .
  ID:   review::.::holistic::error_consistency::viz_broad_exception_swallow::e4a5a44d

  Suggestion: Narrow visualization catches to expected plugin/load errors (`ImportError`, `ValueError`, `TypeError`, `AttributeError`, `OSError`) and preserve exception class/context in warnings; only continue on explicitly recoverable failures.

  [5/5]
  (Tier 1, high confidence)
  ────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────
  Best-effort warnings are rendered with error styling, conflating non-fatal degradation with hard failures.
  Type: Design review (requires judgment)
  File: .
  ID:   review::.::holistic::error_consistency::warning_error_severity_mix::f81ef043

  Suggestion: Separate severity channels: keep hard failures in red and move best-effort degradations to yellow/dim warning style; standardize caller guidance so warnings clearly indicate continuation vs termination.

  North star: strict 90.6/100 → target 98.0 (+7.4 needed)

  Subjective: 15 dimensions below target, 12 stale, 10 review findings open, 218 files need review.

---

# Meta-Reflection Report: `desloppify next` First-Time User Experience

## What I Found

The tool recommended the following as the single highest-priority item:

> **TypedDict-backed payload/state boundaries are softened by `.get()` + default coercion, masking schema drift and invalid runtime shapes.**

- Tier 1, high confidence
- Detector: `review` (holistic), Dimension: `abstraction_fitness`
- Related files: `import_helpers.py`, `scan_workflow.py`
- Suggestion: Introduce strict boundary constructors for `ReviewImportPayload` and `StateModel` slices

The overall strict score is **90.6/100**, target is **98.0** (+7.4 needed). The `Abstraction fit` dimension is at **48.0%**, which the `status` command identified as the second-biggest weighted drag at **-2.03 pts**.

---

## 1. Priority Understanding

**Rating: 5/10**

The `next` output says this is "Tier 1, high confidence" and the `--explain` flag adds: `ranked by tier=1, confidence=high, count=0, id=...`. I understood it was top priority because it's Tier 1. However:

- The tier system itself was never explained in the `next` output. I had to run `plan` separately to discover that Tier 1 means "Auto-fixable (imports, logs, dead deprecated)" -- but confusingly, this finding is labelled "Design review (requires judgment)", which directly contradicts the Tier 1 definition of "auto-fixable." This was genuinely confusing. Either the tier label is wrong for review findings, or the tier descriptions in `plan` don't account for review findings being promoted to T1.
- The `--explain` flag was nearly useless. It just restated the sort key (`tier=1, confidence=high, count=0`) rather than explaining *why* this finding matters more than the 748 other open items. I expected something like "This finding is T1 because the abstraction_fitness dimension is at 48% (your worst dimension) and this finding directly contributes to that score."
- I never got a clear answer to "why is *this specific* T1 item ranked above the other 5 T1 items?"

## 2. Impact Clarity

**Rating: 3/10**

This was the weakest area. The tool provides:

- A "North star" line: `strict 90.6/100 -> target 98.0 (+7.4 needed)` -- this is the *total* gap, not the impact of fixing this one item.
- No per-finding score impact estimate. There is no line like "fixing this is worth ~X pts."
- The `status` command shows that `Abstraction fit` contributes a -2.03 pt drag, but it has 4 open issues. I cannot determine what fraction of that drag this single finding accounts for.
- The JSON output shows `"impact": 0.0` for all the lowest dimensions, which appears to be a bug or unimplemented field, since "0.0 impact" is clearly not accurate for a dimension at 48%.

To answer "If I fixed this one thing, how much would the score improve?" -- **the information is not available.** I would have to fix it, re-scan, and see. This is a major gap. Even a rough estimate ("resolving this finding could improve Abstraction fit from 48% to ~60%, worth approximately +0.5 pts on strict score") would be extremely valuable.

## 3. Actionability

**Rating: 5/10**

The suggestion was reasonably specific: "Introduce strict boundary constructors for `ReviewImportPayload` and `StateModel` slices: validate required keys explicitly, fail with structured errors on invalid shape, and reserve default insertion for truly optional fields only."

The JSON output includes evidence with line numbers:
- `_normalize_import_payload_shape()` reads required fields via `.get()` (import_helpers.py:58-87)
- Auto-replacement of non-dict state in scan_workflow.py:50-81

However, in the terminal output (which is what most users see), the evidence lines are not shown. Only the one-line summary and suggestion appear. I would need to use `--format json` or `--code` to get the evidence, but `--code` did not actually show any inline code for this finding (it's a holistic review finding scoped to `.`, not a specific file).

Also, the finding says "Type: Design review (requires judgment)" -- this is helpful. But it's also somewhat daunting: this isn't a quick fix, it requires thoughtful refactoring of validation boundaries.

I could probably fix the issue, but I'd need to read the source code at the mentioned files and lines myself. The tool gave me enough pointers to start.

## 4. Subjective Items

**Rating: 4/10**

The finding *is* a subjective dimension item (abstraction_fitness, from a holistic review). The tool does have some signals:

- The `status` command marks dimensions as `[stale]` and shows `[open issues: N]`
- The `next` output says "Type: Design review (requires judgment)"
- The footer says "Subjective: 15 dimensions below target, 12 stale, 10 review findings open, 218 files need review."

However, **the tool never explicitly warned me that re-review can lower scores.** This is a critical omission for subjective items. If I fix this finding and trigger a re-review, the reviewer might find new issues or score the dimension differently, potentially *lowering* it. I had no idea this was a risk from the output alone.

The distinction between "mechanical" (objective, scan-detectable) and "subjective" (AI-reviewed, judgment-based) is somewhat implied by the dimension table in `status` (which has a "Subjective Measures" header) but is never explicitly explained in the `next` output. A first-time user seeing a review finding alongside mechanical findings would not necessarily understand they operate completely differently.

## 5. Score Context in `next`

**Rating: 6/10**

The `next` output includes:
- `North star: strict 90.6/100 -> target 98.0 (+7.4 needed)` -- this is helpful
- The tier navigator: `Open: T1:6 T2:89 T3:405 T4:248` -- helpful for understanding scale

But it does NOT include:
- The current dimension scores (I had to run `status` separately to see that Abstraction fit is at 48%)
- What "strict" means vs. "overall" vs. "objective" (I had to run `status` to get the one-line guide: "overall (lenient) / objective (mechanical only) / strict (penalizes wontfix) / verified (scan-confirmed only)")
- Any explanation of what the tiers mean

The `status` command's "Score recipe" section was the most illuminating part of the entire experience -- it showed me exactly how the 90.6 is computed (40% mechanical + 60% subjective) and listed the biggest weighted drags. This information should arguably be surfaced (or at least referenced) in `next`.

## 6. Resolution Flow

**Rating: 7/10**

The `next` output shows the exact `desloppify resolve fixed` and `desloppify resolve wontfix` commands to run, complete with the finding ID pre-filled. The `--attest` flag with the placeholder text is a nice anti-gaming mechanism.

What was less clear:
- Do I need to re-scan after resolving? The tool warns "Tool code changed since last scan... Consider re-running: desloppify scan" but doesn't say whether `resolve` triggers a re-scan.
- For subjective findings, do I need to also trigger a re-review after fixing? The `show subjective` output suggests running `desloppify review --run-batches...` but it's not clear if that's part of the resolution flow or separate.
- The attestation placeholder text says "I have actually [DESCRIBE THE CONCRETE CHANGE YOU MADE] and I am not gaming the score by resolving without fixing." -- the square bracket placeholder is clear enough, though a first-time user might wonder if they need to keep the surrounding text verbatim or replace the whole thing.

## 7. Confusing Moments

1. **Tier 1 = "Auto-fixable" but the finding says "Design review (requires judgment)"**: The `plan` output describes Tier 1 as "Auto-fixable (imports, logs, dead deprecated)" with "97% addressed." But all 6 T1 items are review findings requiring judgment. This is a direct contradiction that undermined my trust in the tier system.

2. **`--explain` was nearly identical to default output**: The only addition was `explain: ranked by tier=1, confidence=high, count=0, id=...`. I expected a paragraph of reasoning. This felt like a debug trace, not an explanation.

3. **`show abstraction_fitness` returned "No open findings matching: abstraction_fitness"**: The finding's dimension is `abstraction_fitness`, but I couldn't filter by it. The tool suggested using detector names (logs, unused, exports...) instead. Dimensions and detectors are apparently different namespaces, which was not obvious.

4. **`"impact": 0.0` in JSON output**: All three "lowest dimensions" show zero impact. Either this field is unimplemented or I'm misunderstanding what "impact" means here. It's misleading to show a number that appears meaningless.

5. **"File: ." for a holistic finding**: The finding is scoped to the entire codebase (`.`), but the output shows `File: .` as if it's a specific file. This was momentarily confusing -- is it literally the file `.`? Is it the root directory? The `related files` in the JSON were much more useful.

6. **"Score plateaued at 90.6 for 5 scans"**: This appeared in the narrative headline in `status` but not in `next`. Since `next` is where I'm deciding what to work on, knowing about the plateau there would add urgency.

7. **No per-finding score impact anywhere**: I ran `next`, `next --explain`, `show`, `status`, `plan`, and `next --format json`. None of them told me "fixing this one finding is worth approximately X points." This was the single most important question I had, and it went unanswered.

## 8. Overall UX Rating

**Rating: 5.5/10** for a first-time agent user.

The tool does several things well:
- Clear, pre-filled resolve commands
- The `status` command's "Score recipe" and "Biggest weighted drags" section is excellent
- The tier navigator gives a quick sense of scale
- Evidence with line numbers in JSON format is valuable

**The single change that would improve it most**: Add a per-finding estimated score impact to the `next` output. Something like:

```
Score impact: Resolving this finding could improve Abstraction fit from 48% -> ~55%,
              raising strict score by approximately +0.3 pts (90.6 -> ~90.9)
```

Even a rough estimate (based on how many open findings exist in the dimension and their relative weights) would dramatically help users prioritize. Right now, the tool tells me *what* to work on but not *how much it matters quantitatively*, which is the key question for any agent or developer deciding how to spend their time.

**Runner-up improvement**: Make `--explain` actually explain. It should answer "Why this item and not the 748 others?" with dimension context, score impact, and the logical chain from "this dimension is dragging your score" to "this finding is the top-ranked item in that dimension."

# Meta-Reflection Report: Desloppify Status/Plan UX Evaluation

## 1. Dashboard Comprehension (`status`)

**Rating: 7/10**

The `status` command produces a dense but information-rich dashboard. At a glance, I could see:
- Four scores at the top (overall, objective, strict, verified)
- A dimension table with health percentages and bar charts
- A structural debt breakdown by directory area
- Agent-oriented "next steps" coaching at the bottom

What was **clear**: The dimension table is well-structured. The progress bars give an instant visual read on which dimensions are healthy (green) vs struggling. I could immediately see that "Abstraction fit" at 48.0% is the worst dimension. The "Biggest weighted drags" section at the bottom was excellent -- it directly answered "what's holding the score back?"

What was **not clear**: The sheer volume of output was overwhelming on first read. The dashboard mixes three different audiences in one output: (a) a high-level scorecard for a human, (b) an agent-oriented work plan with specific `desloppify show` commands, and (c) a structural debt decomposition for sub-agent farming. As a new user, I did not know which section to focus on. The "Structural Debt by Area" table was confusing -- what does "Weight" mean? What does "Debt: 0" mean when there are clearly hundreds of open items?

The counts at top -- `open (in-scope): 733 · open (out-of-scope carried): 3680 · open (global): 4413` -- were puzzling. What is "out-of-scope carried"? Why are there 3680 out-of-scope findings? This was not explained.

## 2. Score Legend in Status

**Rating: 8/10**

The score guide line is:

> `Score guide: overall (lenient) · objective (mechanical only) · strict (penalizes wontfix) · verified (scan-confirmed only)`

This is present and helpful. In one line I learned that:
- "overall" is the lenient view
- "objective" only counts mechanical/automated checks
- "strict" penalizes wontfix items
- "verified" only counts scan-confirmed things

The follow-up line `Focus on strict — it's your north star.` is excellent coaching. It tells me which number matters.

What would improve it: The parenthetical descriptions are very terse. "lenient" -- lenient how? "penalizes wontfix" -- by how much? "scan-confirmed only" -- as opposed to what? A new user might wonder what "verified" means vs "objective" since both seem to be about confirmed/mechanical things. The `Score recipe` section later clarifies that overall = 40% mechanical + 60% subjective, which is very helpful, but it's far down in the output. If the recipe were closer to the score guide, it would make the top-line scores much more understandable immediately.

## 3. Dimension Understanding (Mechanical vs Subjective)

**Rating: 6/10**

The status output does visually separate them with a divider line:
> `── Subjective Measures (matches scorecard.png) ──────────────────────`

This tells me there are two categories, and the subjective ones match some scorecard image. The mechanical dimensions (File health, Code quality, Duplication, Test health, Security) have actual check counts in the "Checks" column, while subjective ones show `—`. That is an implicit signal but not explicitly explained.

What I had to **infer**:
- Mechanical dimensions are computed automatically from code analysis (they have check counts)
- Subjective dimensions come from some "review" process (their Action column says "review")
- The `[stale]` tags on subjective dimensions mean they haven't been re-reviewed recently
- The `[open issues: N]` notation on subjective rows relates to review findings

What was **never explained**: How subjective scores are computed. Is 48.0% for "Abstraction fit" based on 4 open issues? If so, what's the denominator? The bottom legend says `Health = open penalized | Strict = open + wontfix penalized` but for subjective dimensions with `—` checks, the health calculation is opaque.

## 4. Subjective Dimension Access

**Rating: 3/10**

This was the most frustrating part of the experience. When I ran:

```
python -m desloppify --lang python show naming_quality
```

I got:
> `No open findings matching: naming_quality`
> `Try: show <detector>, show <file>, or show subjective. Detectors: logs, unused, exports, deprecated, structural, props, single_use, coupling, cycles, orphaned, ...`

The tool gave me zero explanation of why this didn't work. It didn't say "naming_quality is a subjective dimension, not a detector." It didn't say "subjective dimensions don't have individual findings." It just said "no findings" and suggested I try a detector name instead.

I then tried `show abstraction_fitness` (the worst dimension) and got the same result. The tool listed available detectors but did not list subjective dimension names. A user seeing "Abstraction fit 48.0%" in the status dashboard and then trying `show abstraction_fit` or `show abstraction_fitness` gets a dead end with no guidance.

Eventually I learned that `show review --status open` reveals the review findings that feed into subjective scores. But that mapping (subjective dimension name -> review findings with `holistic::abstraction_fitness` in their IDs) was something I had to piece together myself.

**What would help enormously**: If `show naming_quality` recognized it as a subjective dimension and either (a) showed the current score + how it was computed + the review findings tagged with that dimension, or (b) showed a clear message like: "naming_quality is a subjective dimension scored at 90.0%. It has 0 open review findings. Run `desloppify review --prepare --dimensions naming_quality` to re-assess it."

## 5. Plan Usefulness

**Rating: 7/10**

The `plan` command added significant value over `status`:
- **Tier breakdown with percentages**: `Tier 1: 6 open / 172 total (97% addressed)` through `Tier 4: 233 open / 576 total (60% addressed)` gave me a clear sense of how much work remained at each priority level.
- **Concrete file-level findings**: Plan lists every open finding organized by tier and file, with checkbox-style formatting. This is directly actionable.
- **Tier definitions**: Each tier has a parenthetical description (e.g., "Auto-fixable (imports, logs, dead deprecated)") that explains what belongs there.

What plan added over status: Status tells me "Code quality is at 96.9%." Plan tells me exactly which 214 findings are dragging it down, organized by priority. It turns a dashboard into a work queue.

Limitation: The output was 157KB -- too large to read in one sitting. For a large codebase, the full plan is more of a reference document than something you read top-to-bottom. The tiered summary at the top was the most useful part.

## 6. Wontfix Visibility

**Rating: 5/10**

The status output mentions wontfix in several places:
- Score guide: `strict (penalizes wontfix)`
- Legend: `Health = open penalized | Strict = open + wontfix penalized`
- Plan header: `0 wontfix`

Since this codebase has 0 wontfix items, Health and Strict are identical for every dimension. This makes it hard to understand the distinction in practice. The concept is mentioned but not demonstrated.

What I understood: "wontfix" is a status you can assign to findings you choose not to fix. The "strict" score penalizes you for those, while the regular "health" score doesn't. This is a reasonable concept -- it measures "honest debt" vs "comfortable debt."

What was unclear: How much does a wontfix item penalize strict? Is it the same as an open item, or is there a fractional penalty? The `Score recipe` section explains the mechanical/subjective weighting but not the wontfix penalty formula.

## 7. Navigation Flow (Status -> Understanding -> Fixing)

**Rating: 6/10**

The flow I experienced:
1. `status` -- See the big picture. Identified "Abstraction fit 48.0%" as the worst.
2. Tried `show abstraction_fitness` -- Dead end. No findings, no guidance.
3. Tried `show subjective` -- "No open findings matching: subjective" but got a hint about review.
4. Tried `show review --status open` -- Found the actual review findings tagged with `abstraction_fitness`.
5. `next --count 5` -- Got the top 5 items with clear suggestions.

The gap between steps 1 and 4 was painful. The status dashboard names dimensions in display format ("Abstraction fit") but doesn't provide the internal key (`abstraction_fitness`). Even if I guessed the key, `show` didn't recognize it. The `next` command was the most navigable -- it showed exactly what to work on with actionable suggestions.

The coaching at the bottom of `status` is helpful -- it provides copy-pasteable commands like `desloppify show orphaned --status open` and `desloppify review --prepare`. But these commands are for the agent use case, not for a human who wants to understand what "Abstraction fit 48.0%" actually means.

## 8. Confusing Moments

Every moment I was confused, with exact text:

1. **`open (out-of-scope carried): 3680`** -- What does "out-of-scope carried" mean? Are these findings from files not in the current scan scope? From a previous scan? Why are they carried?

2. **`Tier  Action` columns for subjective dimensions all say `T4  review`** -- Does T4 mean these are low priority? The status dashboard shows "Abstraction fit 48.0%" (the worst dimension) as T4, while "File health 90.7%" is T3. This is counterintuitive. Why is the worst-scoring dimension at a lower priority tier than a 90%+ dimension?

3. **`[stale]` tag** -- "12 stale subjective dimensions." What makes a dimension stale? How old is too old? The status says to "re-review" but doesn't say why they became stale or what triggered it.

4. **`── Structural Debt by Area ──` table columns `Debt` and `Weight`** -- "Debt: 0" for every area, but there are clearly hundreds of open items. What does "Debt" measure if not open findings? And what is "Weight" -- 869 for languages, 406 for app -- weight of what?

5. **`Score plateaued at 90.6 for 5 scans.`** -- Useful information, but how do I know what changed between scans? Is there a history command?

6. **`verified 95.0/100` vs `objective 95.0/100`** -- These are the same number. What's the difference between "verified" and "objective"? The guide says "scan-confirmed only" vs "mechanical only" but those sound like the same thing.

7. **`Create a task doc for each area -> farm to sub-agents for decomposition`** -- This felt agent-specific and confusing for a human user. What task doc? What sub-agents?

8. **`No open findings matching: subjective`** when running `show subjective` -- The tool shows "No findings" then immediately shows subjective quality info below. This is contradictory -- it suggests there are no subjective findings, then talks about subjective dimensions being below target.

## 9. Overall UX Rating

**Rating: 7/10**

The `status` and `plan` commands are information-rich and clearly designed with care. The progress bars, tier system, weighted drag analysis, and copy-pasteable next-step commands are all strong features. The score guide is a good addition. The coaching (north star messaging, agent plan, focus recommendations) shows the tool is opinionated about workflow, which is helpful.

**The single change that would improve it most**: Make subjective dimension names navigable in `show`. When a user runs `show abstraction_fitness` (or `show "Abstraction fit"`), the tool should either:
- Display the dimension's current score, how it was computed, the associated review findings, and a command to re-review it, OR
- At minimum, display a clear message: "abstraction_fitness is a subjective dimension, not a detector. Current score: 48.0%. Run `show review --status open` and look for findings tagged `abstraction_fitness` to see open issues."

Right now, the biggest gap in the information flow is that the status dashboard highlights subjective dimensions as the primary score drags (the "Biggest weighted drags" section), but the `show` command cannot display or navigate to them. This creates a frustrating dead end at exactly the moment the user is most engaged and trying to dig deeper.

## Practical Task Summary

### Overall Health of the Desloppify Codebase

The codebase has an **overall score of 90.6/100**, with a **strict score of 90.6/100** and an **objective (mechanical-only) score of 94.9/100**. This is a 641-file, 126K LOC Python project. It has 739 open findings out of approximately 12,000+ total checks performed across 20 health dimensions.

The mechanical/automated checks (file health, code quality, duplication, test health, security) are mostly in excellent shape -- all above 90% except nothing mechanical is below that threshold. The weaknesses are concentrated in the **subjective/review-based dimensions**, particularly:

- **Abstraction fit: 48.0%** -- by far the lowest dimension, and flagged as the place "where the breakthrough is"
- **Contracts: 65.0%** -- coherence between interfaces and implementations
- **Error consistency: 65.3%** -- inconsistent error handling patterns
- **Stale migration: 81.5%** -- incomplete migrations
- **Logic clarity: 84.1%** -- code readability concerns

### Which Score Matters Most

The `status` command explicitly tells you: **"Focus on strict -- it's your north star."** The `next` command reinforces this with: **"North star: strict 90.6/100 -> target 98.0 (+7.4 needed)"**.

The `status` output also provides a "Score guide" line that explains each score: `overall (lenient) . objective (mechanical only) . strict (penalizes wontfix) . verified (scan-confirmed only)`.

### What to Focus On First

The tool is very clear about prioritization. The `scan` output says: "Score plateaued at 90.6 for 5 scans. Abstraction fit (48.0%) is where the breakthrough is." The **"Score recipe" section** breaks down the biggest weighted drags on the overall score:

1. **Contracts**: -2.05 pts drag (65.0%, 9.8% of subjective pool)
2. **Abstraction fit**: -2.03 pts drag (48.0%, 6.5% of subjective pool)
3. **File health**: -1.24 pts drag (90.7%, 33.3% of mechanical pool)
4. **Type safety**: -0.53 pts drag
5. **Error consistency**: -0.51 pts drag

The `next` command surfaces Tier 1 review items -- all "Design review (requires judgment)" findings related to abstraction fitness, contract coherence, and error consistency. These are the highest-impact items.

### Following the Tool's Suggestions

The tool consistently recommended:
1. `desloppify next` -- done, surfaced 5 Tier 1 items (all review findings requiring design judgment)
2. `desloppify status` -- done, got the full dashboard with score guide and dimension table
3. `desloppify show review --status open` -- done, showed 10 concrete review findings with specific suggestions
4. `desloppify show --chronic` -- done, showed 178 "chronic reopeners" (findings that keep coming back)
5. Running `desloppify review --run-batches ...` to refresh stale subjective dimensions -- not run (requires external LLM runner)

## Meta-Reflection Report

### 1. Score Comprehension

When I first saw the four scores in the scan output -- `overall 90.6/100  objective 94.9/100 (-0.1)  strict 90.6/100  verified 94.9/100 (-0.1)` -- I did **not** immediately understand what each one meant. The scan output itself provides no legend for these scores. They are just listed as bare numbers.

However, when I ran `desloppify status`, it provided the critical line:
```
Score guide: overall (lenient) · objective (mechanical only) · strict (penalizes wontfix) · verified (scan-confirmed only)
```

This was helpful but still somewhat opaque. "Lenient" compared to what? "Scan-confirmed only" -- as opposed to what kind of unconfirmed findings? I could infer that "objective" means automated/tool-detected only (no subjective review), and "strict" penalizes wontfix resolutions (so findings you mark as "won't fix" still count against you). But the distinction between "overall" and "strict" and between "objective" and "verified" required me to reason about it.

The Score recipe section (40% mechanical + 60% subjective) was extremely valuable for understanding what drives the overall score.

**Clarity rating: 6/10.** The `status` command's score guide is the key, but it only appears there, not in the scan output where you first see the scores. The short descriptions like "lenient" and "scan-confirmed only" need more context for a new user.

### 2. North Star Clarity

Yes, I figured it out. It was **reasonably clear but required running two commands**. The `status` command says explicitly: "Focus on strict -- it's your north star." The `next` command says: "North star: strict 90.6/100 -> target 98.0 (+7.4 needed)."

However, this information is **not present in the scan output itself**. The scan output just lists four scores with no indication of which one matters. If a user only runs `scan` and stops there, they would not know which score to focus on.

**How I would improve this**: Put the "Focus on strict -- it's your north star" text directly in the scan output, right next to the scores line.

### 3. Next Action Clarity

After reading the scan output, I knew exactly what to do. The last few lines of the scan output are a clear call to action:

```
→ 59 security findings — review before other cleanup. Score plateaued at 90.6 for 5 scans. Abstraction fit (48.0%) is where the breakthrough is.
Run `desloppify next` for the highest-priority item.
Run `desloppify status` for the full dashboard.
```

Each subsequent command (`next`, `status`, `show`) also ends with concrete "run this command" suggestions. The workflow chains together naturally: scan -> status/next -> show -> resolve.

**Clarity rating: 9/10.** The suggested next commands are always present, always concrete, and always contextually relevant. The only minor issue is that the scan output simultaneously tells you to review security findings first AND that abstraction fit is where the breakthrough is -- two potentially conflicting priorities. But `next` resolves this by showing the actual highest-priority item.

### 4. Confusing Moments

1. **"610 findings hidden (showing 10/detector)"** -- I initially didn't understand why findings were being hidden. It took me a moment to realize this is a noise-reduction feature. The text could say "610 findings hidden for readability" or similar.

2. **"[stale -- re-review]"** next to dimension scores -- I didn't initially know what "stale" meant. Does it mean the score is unreliable? Outdated? After seeing the 12-dimension re-review command suggested at the bottom, I inferred it means the subjective review data is old and needs to be refreshed. But the term "stale" is ambiguous on first read.

3. **"open (in-scope): 739 . open (out-of-scope carried): 3680 . open (global): 4419"** from the `status` command -- What is "out-of-scope carried"? Why are there 3680 findings that are "out of scope"? This was never explained. Are these findings from other scans? From excluded paths?

4. **The Tier system (T1-T4)** -- The plan command provides brief descriptions: "T1: Auto-fixable", "T2: Quick fixes", "T3: Needs judgment", "T4: Major refactors". But in the scan and status output, tiers are used without explanation. I had to run `plan` to learn what T1-T4 mean.

5. **"Noise budget: 10/detector (49 hidden: security: +49)"** -- The term "noise budget" was initially confusing. I gathered it means "we show at most 10 findings per detector to avoid overwhelming output," but the term "budget" is unusual here.

6. **The scorecard image shows two percentages per dimension** (e.g., "90.7% 90.7%") -- I eventually understood these are "Health" and "Strict" scores, but the image has no column headers, so on first glance the double numbers are mysterious.

7. **"Skipped auto-resolve for: review (returned 0 -- likely transient)"** -- This appeared in the scan output and I had no idea what it meant. What is auto-resolve? Why would returning 0 be transient? This seems like an internal diagnostic message that shouldn't face the user.

8. **"Weight" column in Structural Debt table** -- The status command shows a "Weight" column with values like 869, 421, 293. No explanation of what the weight represents or how it's calculated.

### 5. Missing Information

1. **No explanation of how dimension scores are calculated.** I know the overall score is 40% mechanical + 60% subjective, but how is each individual dimension score (e.g., "Abstraction fit: 48.0%") computed? What does 48% mean -- 48% of checks passed? 48% of some weighted formula?

2. **No explanation of what "findings" are resolved vs. open vs. wontfix.** The plan says "11328 auto-resolved" but never explains what auto-resolution means or what triggers it.

3. **No definition of "subjective" vs. "mechanical" dimensions.** I could infer from context that "mechanical" means automated tool checks and "subjective" means LLM-based review, but this is never stated explicitly.

4. **No historical trend.** The output says "Score plateaued at 90.6 for 5 scans" which is great, but there is no history graph or table showing how the score has changed over time. I would have liked to see "90.6 -> 90.6 -> 90.6 -> 90.6 -> 90.6" or similar.

5. **No estimate of effort.** When it says "Abstraction fit is where the breakthrough is," I would like to know: how much effort to improve it? How many points would I gain by fixing the 4 open abstraction fit findings?

### 6. Wontfix Understanding

I **partially** understood wontfix from the tool output. Here is what I pieced together:

- The `status` command's score guide says: `strict (penalizes wontfix)`. So wontfix findings still count against the strict score.
- The `resolve` command help shows `wontfix` as a resolution status alongside `fixed`, `open`, and `false_positive`.
- The `status` table has both "Health" and "Strict" columns, with the footer: `Health = open penalized | Strict = open + wontfix penalized`.
- The `next` command's resolve suggestion shows both `resolve fixed` and `resolve wontfix` as options, with the attestation requirement.

So wontfix means "I acknowledge this finding but am choosing not to fix it." The `overall/health` score ignores wontfixed items (treats them as resolved), but the `strict` score still penalizes them. Since strict is the "north star," wontfixing things does not actually help you reach the target.

This was **not obvious** from the scan output alone. I had to run `status` and read the footer text to understand the Health vs. Strict distinction. The `resolve --help` output also helped -- the `--attest` flag with its anti-gaming language makes it clear that wontfix is a legitimate but tracked resolution path.

**Where I learned this**: Primarily from the `status` command's "Score guide" line and the table footer `Health = open penalized | Strict = open + wontfix penalized`. The `resolve --help` attestation text also helped contextualize the design intent.

### 7. Overall UX Rating

**Rating: 7.5/10** for a first-time agent user.

**Strengths:**
- The workflow chain is excellent: every command ends with "run this next" suggestions
- The `next` command is perfectly designed -- gives you exactly one thing to focus on with full context
- The Score recipe breakdown showing "biggest weighted drags" is extremely valuable for prioritization
- The tier system (T1-T4) with descriptions is a good framework once you discover it via `plan`
- The noise budget / 10-per-detector limit keeps output manageable
- Chronic reopeners are tracked and surfaced with clear "fix properly or wontfix" guidance
- The attestation requirement for wontfix is a clever anti-gaming mechanism

**Weaknesses:**
- The scan output (the first thing you see) is the least well-explained output -- no score legend, no tier definitions, no north star indicator
- Jargon like "stale," "noise budget," "out-of-scope carried," and "auto-resolve" is used without definition
- The four scores are all shown upfront but not distinguished until you run a different command
- Some internal diagnostic messages ("Skipped auto-resolve for: review (returned 0)") leak into user-facing output

**Single change that would improve it most**: Add a 3-line "Score guide" block directly to the scan output, right after the scores line. Something like:

```
Scores: overall 90.6/100  objective 94.9/100  strict 90.6/100  verified 94.9/100
  ↑ Focus on strict (your north star). It penalizes both open issues and wontfix.
  overall = 40% mechanical + 60% subjective | objective = mechanical only | verified = scan-confirmed only
```

This single addition would eliminate the biggest first-time-user confusion: seeing four numbers with no indication of what they mean or which one matters.

Desloppify — codebase health tracker

positional arguments:
  {scan,status,tree,show,next,resolve,ignore,fix,plan,viz,detect,move,review,zone,config,dev,langs,update-skill}
    scan                Run all detectors, update state, show diff
    status              Score dashboard with per-tier progress
    tree                Annotated codebase tree (text)
    show                Dig into findings by file, directory, detector, or ID
    next                Show next highest-priority open finding
    resolve             Mark finding(s) as fixed/wontfix/false_positive/open
    ignore              Add pattern to ignore list, remove matching findings
    fix                 Auto-fix mechanical issues
    plan                Generate prioritized markdown plan from state
    viz                 Generate interactive HTML treemap
    detect              Run a single detector directly (bypass state)
    move                Move a file or directory and update all import
                        references
    review              Prepare or import holistic subjective review
    zone                Show/set/clear zone classifications
    config              Show/set/unset project configuration
    dev                 Developer utilities
    langs               List all available language plugins with depth and
                        tools
    update-skill        Install or update the desloppify skill/agent document

options:
  -h, --help            show this help message and exit
  --lang LANG           Language to scan (bash, clojure, csharp, cxx, dart,
                        elixir, erlang, fsharp, gdscript, go, haskell, java,
                        javascript, kotlin, lua, nim, ocaml, perl, php,
                        powershell, python, r, ruby, rust, scala, swift,
                        typescript, zig). Auto-detected if omitted.
  --exclude PATTERN     Path pattern to exclude (component/prefix match;
                        repeatable)

workflow:
  scan                          Run all detectors, update state, show diff
  status                        Score dashboard with per-tier progress
  tree                          Annotated codebase tree (zoom with --focus)
  show <pattern>                Dig into findings by file/dir/detector/ID
  resolve <pattern> <status>    Mark findings as open/fixed/wontfix/false_positive
  ignore <pattern>              Suppress findings matching a pattern
  zone show                     Show zone classifications for all files
  zone set <file> <zone>        Override zone for a file
  review --prepare              Prepare holistic codebase review data
  review --import FILE          Import review findings from JSON
  review --validate-import FILE Validate import payload/trust mode without mutating state
  review --external-start       Start external blind review session (Claude cloud)
  review --external-submit      Submit external session results with canonical provenance
  review --merge                Merge duplicate review findings
  plan                          Generate prioritized markdown plan

examples:
  desloppify scan --skip-slow
  desloppify --lang python scan --path scripts/desloppify
  desloppify tree --focus shared/components --sort findings --depth 3
  desloppify tree --detail --focus shared/components/MediaLightbox --min-loc 300
  desloppify show src/shared/components/PromptEditorModal.tsx
  desloppify show gods
  desloppify show "src/shared/components/MediaLightbox"
  desloppify resolve fixed "unused::src/foo.tsx::React" --note "removed unused React import" --attest "I have actually removed the unused React import from foo.tsx and I am not gaming the score by resolving without fixing."
  desloppify resolve fixed "logs::src/foo.tsx::*" --note "removed debug logs" --attest "I have actually removed all console.log calls from foo.tsx and I am not gaming the score by resolving without fixing."
  desloppify resolve wontfix deprecated --note "migration in progress" --attest "I have actually verified these are tracked in the migration plan and I am not gaming the score by resolving without fixing."
  desloppify ignore "smells::*::async_no_await" --attest "I have actually verified these are intentional fire-and-forget patterns and I am not gaming the score by resolving without fixing."
  desloppify detect logs --top 10
  desloppify detect dupes --threshold 0.9
  desloppify review --validate-import findings.json --attested-external --attest "I validated this review was completed without awareness of overall score and is unbiased."
  desloppify review --external-start --external-runner claude
  desloppify review --external-submit --session-id <session_id> --import findings.json
  desloppify review --run-batches --runner codex --parallel --scan-after-import --retrospective
  desloppify dev scaffold-lang go --extension .go --marker go.mod --default-src .
  desloppify move src/shared/hooks/useFoo.ts src/shared/hooks/video/useFoo.ts --dry-run
  desloppify move scripts/foo/bar.py scripts/foo/baz/bar.py

# Meta-Reflection Report: Desloppify UX from a Score-Maximizing Agent's Perspective

## 1. Goal Clarity: Which Score to Maximize?

**Rating: 8/10**

The tool is quite explicit about which score matters. The `status` output says directly:

> "Focus on strict -- it's your north star."

And the `next` output reinforces this at the bottom:

> "North star: strict 90.6/100 -> target 98.0 (+7.4 needed)"

The `config show` also reveals:

> "target_strict_score 98 -- North-star strict score target used to prioritize guidance"

What works well: The score guide line in status is clear: `"Score guide: overall (lenient) . objective (mechanical only) . strict (penalizes wontfix) . verified (scan-confirmed only)"`. This immediately tells me overall is lenient, strict is what matters, and wontfix hurts strict.

What is slightly confusing: There are 4 scores displayed (overall, objective, strict, verified), and on my first read, having 4 numbers at once created brief cognitive overload. Currently `Health` and `Strict` columns appear in the dimension table -- but for my codebase they are identical (both 90.7%, 96.9%, etc.) because there are 0 wontfix items. A new user seeing two identical columns may wonder why both exist. The difference would only become apparent after wontfixing something.

## 2. Strategy Formation

**Rating: 7/10**

The tool gives several strategy hints. The `status` output includes a "Score recipe" section:

> "overall = 40% mechanical + 60% subjective"
> "Biggest weighted drags: Contracts: -2.05 pts, Abstraction fit: -2.03 pts, File health: -1.24 pts..."

This is extremely useful -- it tells me exactly which dimensions drag the score most. I can immediately see that improving Abstraction fit from 48% would have outsized impact.

The `status` output also says:

> "Score plateaued at 90.6 for 5 scans. Abstraction fit (48.0%) is where the breakthrough is."

This is a strong, opinionated coaching signal and it is helpful.

However, strategy formation was weakened by:
- The `next` command's top 5 items are all "Design review (requires judgment)" items. As an agent, these are the hardest to act on mechanically. I would have preferred seeing the tool say "Here are 3 things you can auto-fix RIGHT NOW, and here are 2 high-impact items that need design judgment."
- The tier system (T1-T4) labels are helpful but the actual T1 items are review findings, not mechanical auto-fixes. The tier description says "Auto-fixable (imports, logs, dead deprecated)" but 5 of 6 T1 items are holistic review findings that require judgment. This is confusing -- those don't feel "auto-fixable."
- There is no single "if you do these 5 things your score goes from 90.6 to X" projection.

## 3. Impact Visibility (Score Impact Per Fix)

**Rating: 4/10**

This is the weakest area. The tool does NOT show per-item score impact. When I look at a finding in `next`, I see:

> "Dimension: Code quality -- 96.9% (strict: 96.9%) (217 of 3,824 checks failing)"

This tells me the dimension and its current health, but not "fixing this item would move your strict score by X points." I can do rough math: each Code quality finding is ~1/3824 of that dimension's pool, then weighted into overall. But the tool does not do this math for me.

The "Biggest weighted drags" section in `status` is a good aggregate view, but I want per-item: "This finding is worth 0.03 pts toward strict" so I can triage efficiently.

For subjective dimensions (like Abstraction fit at 48%), there is no indication of how much fixing one review finding would move the score. The dimension has 4 open issues, but would fixing all 4 move it from 48% to 60%? 80%? 100%? There is no projection.

## 4. Wontfix Honesty

**Rating: 7/10**

The tool communicates clearly that wontfix hurts strict score:

> "Health = open penalized | Strict = open + wontfix penalized"

The resolve command requires an `--attest` flag with specific keywords "I have actually" and "not gaming" -- a friction-based anti-gaming measure. The template reads:

> `--attest "I have actually [DESCRIBE THE CONCRETE CHANGE YOU MADE] and I am not gaming the score by resolving without fixing."`

There is also a `--confirm-batch-wontfix` flag required when wontfixing more than 10 findings at once, which prevents mass-wontfixing.

What I did NOT see: a warning about what happens to the overall-vs-strict gap when you wontfix things. Currently overall equals strict (90.6), but if I wontfixed 100 items, overall would rise while strict stays flat or drops. The tool does not seem to proactively warn "Your overall/strict gap is X -- hidden debt is accumulating." This would be visible only indirectly if the Health and Strict columns diverged in the dimension table.

## 5. Auto-Fix Discovery

**Rating: 3/10**

This is a major gap. The `fix --help` shows available fixers, but for Python there are "none yet." For TypeScript there are 6 fixers (dead-useeffect, debug-logs, empty-if-chain, unused-imports, unused-params, unused-vars).

But the critical issue: the `next` output and `show` output never tag items with "[auto]" or "auto-fixable." I see `[script]` tags on some findings (which I believe indicate script/zone classification, not auto-fixability). The tier descriptions say "Tier 1: Auto-fixable" but the actual T1 items are review findings requiring judgment.

I would expect to see something like:
- "[auto-fixable: `desloppify fix unused-imports`]" on individual findings
- A count of "X items have auto-fixers available" in status
- The `next` command suggesting `fix` commands where applicable

The only unused-import finding (`typing.Any` in `scan_coverage.py`) is T1 and does have an auto-fix in TypeScript but not in Python. For Python, the tool doesn't tell me how to fix it -- just shows the finding.

## 6. Subjective vs Mechanical Distinction

**Rating: 7/10**

The distinction is communicated, but requires careful reading. In `status`:

> "-- Subjective Measures (matches scorecard.png) --"

This header separates mechanical dimensions (File health, Code quality, Duplication, Test health, Security) from subjective ones (AI generated debt, Abstraction fit, Contracts, etc.).

The "Action" column uses different verbs: `fix` for Code quality, `refactor` for File health/Duplication, `move` for Security, and `review` for all subjective dimensions. The `review` action implies human judgment.

The score recipe makes the split explicit: "overall = 40% mechanical + 60% subjective."

What helped most: the `[stale]` tags on subjective dimensions, which tell me those need re-review. The tool suggests:
> "12 stale subjective dimensions -- run `desloppify review --prepare --dimensions ...` to re-review"

What was less clear: I never found a description of what "mechanical" means vs "subjective" in the CLI output itself. I inferred it from context.

## 7. Score Model Transparency

**Rating: 8/10**

The score model is explained directly in `status`:

> "Score recipe: overall = 40% mechanical + 60% subjective"
> "Pool averages: mechanical 94.9% . subjective 87.7%"

And the "Biggest weighted drags" section shows exactly how each dimension contributes negatively. For example:
> "Contracts: -2.05 pts (score 65.0%, 9.8% of subjective pool)"

This tells me: Contracts is 65% healthy, it is 9.8% of the subjective pool (which is 60% of overall), and it drags the overall score by 2.05 points. This is excellent transparency.

I learned this entirely from `status` output. The `plan` command shows the same dimension table but without the score recipe math.

What is missing: I still do not know exactly how "strict" score differs from "overall" in its computation. I know "strict penalizes wontfix" but not the exact formula (e.g., does it count wontfix items as still-open? Or is there a fractional penalty?). As a score-maximizing agent, I need to know this to model the cost of wontfix.

## 8. Confusing Moments

1. **Tier 1 mislabeling**: Tier 1 is described as "Auto-fixable (imports, logs, dead deprecated)" but contains 5 review findings that require design judgment. Only 1 of 6 T1 items is a mechanical unused-import finding. This was the most confusing moment -- I expected T1 to be things I could fix in seconds.

2. **"Noise budget" hiding findings**: The output `"Noise budget: 10/detector (139 hidden: smells: +139)"` means I only see 10 of 149 smell findings. This is reasonable UX but I initially wasn't sure if the hidden items also count toward the score. They presumably do, but the tool doesn't say "these 139 hidden findings also impact your score."

3. **No per-finding score impact**: Every `next` item shows `"Dimension: Code quality -- 96.9% (217 of 3,824 checks failing)"` but never says "fixing this is worth approximately X.XX points." I had to manually reason about this.

4. **`show subjective` returns nothing**: Running `show subjective` returned "No open findings matching: subjective" even though there are clearly subjective dimension issues. The pattern matching doesn't recognize "subjective" as a filter -- you need to use specific dimension names or "review."

5. **Health vs Strict columns identical**: With 0 wontfix items, Health and Strict columns show identical numbers across all dimensions. A new user might wonder why both columns exist. A footnote says `"Health = open penalized | Strict = open + wontfix penalized"` but with no wontfix items, this distinction is invisible.

6. **`[script]` tags**: Many findings are tagged `[script]` (e.g., `"[medium] 1x Monster function (>150 LOC) [script]"`). I initially thought this meant "auto-fixable by script" but it actually refers to the file's zone classification.

## 9. Gaming Resistance

**Rating: 8/10**

The tool has several anti-gaming mechanisms:

1. **Attestation requirement**: `--attest "I have actually [DESCRIBE THE CONCRETE CHANGE YOU MADE] and I am not gaming the score by resolving without fixing."` -- forces the user to claim they actually did something.

2. **Batch wontfix confirmation**: `--confirm-batch-wontfix` required for >10 wontfixes at once.

3. **Strict score penalizes wontfix**: The "north star" metric counts wontfix against you, so mass-wontfixing does not improve your target score.

4. **Reopening detection**: Findings show `"Reopened (x2) -- reappeared in scan (was auto_resolved)"` and `"reopened 2 times -- fix properly or wontfix"` for chronic reopeners.

5. **Scan-based verification**: The "verified" score only includes scan-confirmed fixes, so claiming "fixed" without actually changing code would not improve verified score on next scan.

What could be stronger: The attestation is a text string that an LLM agent could trivially fill in with plausible-sounding text without actually doing the work. The mechanism relies on good faith rather than verification. The scan-based re-check partly compensates for this.

## 10. Overall UX Rating

**Rating: 7/10**

The tool is well-designed for a human developer who wants to systematically improve codebase health. The score recipe transparency, tiered prioritization, and coaching messages are all strong.

For a score-maximizing agent specifically:
- **Strengths**: Clear north star (strict), weighted drag analysis, tier system, anti-gaming mechanisms, re-review nudges for stale dimensions
- **Weaknesses**: No per-item score impact estimates, T1 tier mislabeling (review findings labeled as auto-fixable), no auto-fix tagging on individual findings, no "what-if" projection (e.g., "if you fix all T2 items, strict goes to X"), no Python auto-fixers

**Single change that would improve it most**: Add per-finding score impact estimates. Every finding shown in `next` or `show` should include an approximate score delta, e.g., `"Fixing this: strict +0.03 pts (Code quality 96.9% -> 96.93%)"`. This would let agents (and humans) triage by ROI instead of just by tier. The "Biggest weighted drags" section is a great start, but the per-item granularity is what an optimizer needs.

## 7. Score Model Transparency

> `Score recipe:`

This is excellent. It tells me the formula, the pool weights, the per-dimension weight within each pool, and the resulting drag in absolute points. I can verify: 40% * 94.9% + 60% * 87.7% = 37.96 + 52.62 = 90.58, which rounds to the displayed 90.6. The model is transparent and verifiable.

I learned this entirely from `status` output. The `plan` command shows the same dimension table but without the score recipe math.

For a score-maximizing agent, this matters -- I need to model the exact cost of every wontfix decision.

---

## 8. Confusing Moments

**Moment 1 -- Tier 1 mislabeling**: Tier 1 is described as "Auto-fixable (imports, logs, dead deprecated)" but 5 of 6 actual T1 items are holistic review findings saying `"Type: Design review (requires judgment)"`. Only 1 of 6 is a mechanical unused-import finding. This is the single most confusing moment -- I expected T1 to contain things I could fix in seconds, and instead got architectural design issues. I was left wondering whether the tier system was broken or whether "auto-fixable" means something different than I thought.

**Moment 2 -- `[script]` tags**: Many findings are tagged `[script]`, e.g.: `"○ T2 [medium] 1x Monster function (>150 LOC) [script]"`. I initially interpreted this as "fixable by running a script." It actually means the file is classified in the "script" zone. There is no legend or tooltip explaining zone tags. I had to run `zone show` to understand this, and even then the connection was not obvious.

**Moment 3 -- `show subjective` returns nothing**: Running `python -m desloppify --lang python show subjective` returned `"No open findings matching: subjective"` even though the status output shows multiple subjective dimensions with issues. The filter keyword "subjective" does not map to any detector or pattern. The status footer says `"Run desloppify show subjective for details"` -- but that is a recommendation to improve subjective scores, not a working show command.

**Moment 4 -- Noise budget hiding**: The output `"Noise budget: 10/detector (139 hidden: smells: +139)"` means I see 10 of 149 smell findings. I was not sure whether the 139 hidden items count toward the score. They presumably do, but the tool does not confirm this. A note like "(all 149 count toward score; 139 hidden for readability)" would help.

**Moment 5 -- Health vs Strict columns identical**: With 0 wontfix items, Health and Strict columns show identical numbers for every dimension. Both say 90.7%, 96.9%, etc. A new user forming their mental model sees two identical columns and may not understand why both exist until they eventually wontfix something.

**Moment 6 -- Review findings in Tier 1**: The 5 review findings in T1 are all codebase-wide (`File: .`) and require design judgment. But T1 is labeled "Auto-fixable." I searched for any explanation of why review findings land in T1, and found none. It appears the tier assignment for review findings may use different criteria than for mechanical findings, but this is never explained.

---

## 9. Gaming Resistance

**Rating: 8/10**

The tool has multiple layered anti-gaming mechanisms:

1. **Attestation requirement**: The resolve command requires `--attest` with specific keywords. The template forces you to claim concrete action: `"I have actually [DESCRIBE THE CONCRETE CHANGE YOU MADE] and I am not gaming the score by resolving without fixing."` Both keywords "I have actually" and "not gaming" are validated.

2. **Batch wontfix guard**: `--confirm-batch-wontfix` is required when wontfixing more than 10 findings simultaneously. This prevents a single command from mass-resolving hundreds of items.

3. **Strict score penalizes wontfix**: The north star metric counts wontfix against you, so mass-wontfixing does not improve your target score. This is the most effective anti-gaming mechanism because it makes the dominant gaming strategy (wontfix everything) self-defeating.

4. **Reopening detection**: Findings track reopening history: `"Reopened (x2) — reappeared in scan (was auto_resolved)"` and `"⟳ reopened 2 times — fix properly or wontfix"`. This catches "fix" claims that did not actually fix the underlying issue.

5. **Verified score**: The "verified" score (94.9) only includes scan-confirmed results, so claiming "fixed" without changing code would not survive the next scan.

6. **Stale review tracking**: Subjective reviews are marked `[stale]` after 30 days, preventing one-time inflated reviews from persisting indefinitely.

**What could be stronger**: The attestation is a text string that an LLM agent could trivially fill in with plausible-sounding boilerplate without actually doing the work. For example: `--attest "I have actually reviewed this finding and confirmed it is acceptable technical debt and I am not gaming the score by resolving without fixing."` This technically satisfies the keyword check while describing no concrete change. The scan-based re-verification partially compensates, but only for mechanically detectable findings -- subjective dimension scores are not automatically re-verified.

---

## 10. Overall UX Rating

**Rating: 7/10**

The tool is well-designed for a human developer systematically improving codebase health. The score recipe transparency, weighted drag analysis, tier system, coaching messages, and anti-gaming mechanisms are all strong. The "north star: strict" messaging is clear and consistent.

**Strengths for a score-maximizing agent**:
- Clear north star identification with explicit target (strict 90.6 -> 98.0)
- Score recipe with pool weights and per-dimension drag in absolute points
- Tiered work queue with open counts per tier
- Structural debt breakdown by directory area
- Stale review tracking with suggested re-review commands
- Anti-gaming attestation and batch guards

**Weaknesses for a score-maximizing agent**:
- No per-item score impact estimates (the single biggest gap)
- No "what-if" projection for bulk actions
- T1 tier contains design-judgment items mislabeled as "auto-fixable"
- No auto-fix tagging on individual findings
- No Python auto-fixers, and this is not surfaced prominently
- Strict score formula not shown (only overall formula is given)
- Some navigation dead ends (`show subjective` returns nothing)

**The single change that would improve it most**: Add per-finding score impact estimates. Every finding shown in `next` or `show` should include an approximate score delta, such as: `"Fixing this: strict +0.03 pts (Code quality 96.9% → 96.93%)"`. For subjective dimensions, include a projection: `"Resolving all 4 abstraction_fitness findings could raise dimension from 48% to ~72%, strict impact: +0.97 pts."` This would let agents and humans triage by return-on-investment rather than relying solely on tier ordering. The "Biggest weighted drags" section is a great foundation -- extending it to per-item granularity would be the highest-leverage improvement.

1→"""Shared plan constants and helpers."""
     2→
     3→from __future__ import annotations
     4→
     5→TIER_LABELS = {
     6→    1: "Auto-fixable (imports, logs, dead deprecated)",
     7→    2: "Quick fixes (unused vars, dead exports, exact dupes, orphaned files, cross-tool imports)",
     8→    3: "Needs judgment (smells, near-dupes, single-use, small cycles, state sync)",
     9→    4: "Major refactors (structural decomposition, large import cycles)",
    10→}
    11→
    12→CONFIDENCE_ORDER = {"high": 0, "medium": 1, "low": 2}
    13→
    14→
    15→def is_subjective_phase(phase) -> bool:
    16→    """Return True when a phase is subjective/review oriented."""
    17→    label = (phase.label or "").lower()
    18→    run_name = getattr(phase.run, "__name__", "").lower()
    19→    return (
    20→        "subjective" in label
    21→        or "review" in label
    22→        or run_name == "phase_subjective_review"
    23→    )
    24→

    18→
    19→ALL_STATUSES = set(finding_status_tokens(include_all=True))
    20→ATTEST_EXAMPLE = (
    21→    "I have actually [DESCRIBE THE CONCRETE CHANGE YOU MADE] "
    22→    "and I am not gaming the score by resolving without fixing."
    23→)
    24→
    25→
    26→def detail_dict(item: dict) -> dict:
    27→    """Return finding detail as a dict; tolerate legacy/non-dict payloads."""
    28→    detail = item.get("detail")
    29→    return detail if isinstance(detail, dict) else {}
    30→
    31→
    32→def status_matches(item_status: str, status_filter: str) -> bool:
    33→    return status_filter == "all" or item_status == status_filter
    34→
    35→
    36→def is_subjective_finding(item: dict) -> bool:
    37→    detector = item.get("detector")
    38→    if detector in {"subjective_assessment"}:
    39→        return True
    40→    if detector == "holistic_review":
    41→        return True
    42→    return False
    43→
    44→
    45→def is_review_finding(item: dict) -> bool:
    46→    return item.get("detector") == "review"
    47→
    48→
    49→def review_finding_weight(item: dict) -> float:
    50→    """Return review issue weight aligned with issues list ordering."""
    51→    confidence = str(item.get("confidence", "low")).lower()
    52→    weight_by_confidence = {
    53→        "high": 1.0,
    54→        "medium": 0.7,
    55→        "low": 0.3,
    56→    }
    57→    weight = weight_by_confidence.get(confidence, 0.3)
    58→    if detail_dict(item).get("holistic"):
    59→        weight *= 10.0
    60→    return float(weight)
    61→
    62→
    63→def scope_matches(item: dict, scope: str | None) -> bool:
    64→    """Apply show-style pattern matching against a queue item."""
    65→    if not scope:
    66→        return True
    67→
    68→    item_id = item.get("id", "")
    69→    detector = item.get("detector", "")
    70→    filepath = item.get("file", "")
    71→    summary = item.get("summary", "")
    72→    dimension = detail_dict(item).get("dimension_name", "")
    73→    kind = item.get("kind", "")
    74→
    75→    if "*" in scope:
    76→        return any(
    77→            fnmatch(candidate, scope)
    78→            for candidate in (item_id, filepath, detector, dimension, summary)
    79→        )
    80→
    81→    if "::" in scope:
    82→        return item_id.startswith(scope)
    83→
    84→    lowered = scope.lower()
    85→    if kind == "subjective_dimension":
    86→        return (
    87→            lowered in item_id.lower()
    88→            or lowered in dimension.lower()
    89→            or lowered in summary.lower()
    90→        )
    91→
    92→    return (
    93→        detector == scope
    94→        or filepath == scope
    95→        or filepath.startswith(scope.rstrip("/") + "/")
    96→    )
    97→
    98→
    99→def slugify(text: str) -> str:
   100→    return re.sub(r"[^a-z0-9_]+", "_", text.lower()).strip("_")
   101→
   102→
   103→def _canonical_subjective_dimension_key(display_name: str) -> str:
   104→    """Map a display label (e.g. 'Mid elegance') to its canonical dimension key."""
   105→    cleaned = display_name.replace(" (subjective)", "").strip()
   106→    target = cleaned.lower()
   107→
   108→    for dim_key, label in DISPLAY_NAMES.items():
   109→        if str(label).lower() == target:
   110→            return str(dim_key)
   111→    return slugify(cleaned)
   112→
   113→
   114→def _subjective_dimension_aliases(display_name: str) -> set[str]:
   115→    """Return normalized aliases used to match display labels with finding dimension keys."""
   116→    cleaned = display_name.replace(" (subjective)", "").strip()
   117→    canonical = _canonical_subjective_dimension_key(cleaned)
   118→    return {
   119→        cleaned.lower(),
   120→        cleaned.replace(" ", "_").lower(),
   121→        slugify(cleaned),
   122→        canonical.lower(),
   123→        slugify(canonical),
   124→    }
   125→
   126→
   127→def supported_fixers_for_item(state: dict, item: dict) -> set[str] | None:
   128→    """Return supported fixers for an item's language when known."""
   129→    lang = str(item.get("lang", "") or "").strip()
   130→    if not lang:
   131→        return None
   132→
   133→    caps = state.get("lang_capabilities", {})
   134→    if not isinstance(caps, dict):
   135→        return None
   136→
   137→    lang_caps = caps.get(lang, {})
   138→    if not isinstance(lang_caps, dict):
   139→        return None
   140→
   141→    fixers = lang_caps.get("fixers")
   142→    if not isinstance(fixers, list):
   143→        return None
   144→    return {fixer for fixer in fixers if isinstance(fixer, str)}
   145→
   146→
   147→def primary_command_for_finding(
   148→    item: dict, *, supported_fixers: set[str] | None = None
   149→) -> str:
   150→    detector = item.get("detector", "")
   151→    meta = DETECTORS.get(detector)
   152→    if meta and meta.action_type == "auto_fix" and meta.fixers:
   153→        available_fixers = [
   154→            fixer
   155→            for fixer in meta.fixers
   156→            if supported_fixers is None or fixer in supported_fixers
   157→        ]
   158→        if available_fixers:
   159→            return f"desloppify fix {available_fixers[0]} --dry-run"
   160→    if detector == "subjective_review":
   161→        if is_holistic_subjective_finding(item):
   162→            return "desloppify review --prepare"
   163→        return "desloppify show subjective"
   164→    return f'desloppify resolve fixed "{item.get("id", "")}" --note "<what you did>" --attest "{ATTEST_EXAMPLE}"'
   165→
   166→
   167→def subjective_strict_scores(state: dict) -> dict[str, float]:
   168→    dim_scores = state.get("dimension_scores", {}) or {}
   169→    if not dim_scores:
   170→        return {}
   171→
   172→    entries = scorecard_subjective_entries(state, dim_scores=dim_scores)
   173→    scores: dict[str, float] = {}
   174→    for entry in entries:
   175→        name = str(entry.get("name", "")).strip()
   176→        if not name:
   177→            continue
   178→        strict_val = float(entry.get("strict", entry.get("score", 100.0)))
   179→        [REDACTED](name)
   180→        aliases = _subjective_dimension_aliases(name)
   181→        for cli_key in entry.get("cli_keys", []):
   182→            key = str(cli_key).strip().lower()
   183→            if not key:
   184→                continue
   185→            aliases.add(key)
   186→            aliases.add(slugify(key))
   187→        aliases.add(dim_key.lower())
   188→        aliases.add(slugify(dim_key))
   189→        for alias in aliases:
   190→            scores[alias] = strict_val
   191→    return scores
   192→
   193→
   194→def build_subjective_items(
   195→    state: dict, findings: dict, *, threshold: float = 100.0
   196→) -> list[dict]:
   197→    """Create synthetic subjective work items (always tier 4)."""
   198→    dim_scores = state.get("dimension_scores", {}) or {}
   199→    if not dim_scores:
   200→        return []
   201→    threshold = max(0.0, min(100.0, float(threshold)))
   202→
   203→    subjective_entries = scorecard_subjective_entries(state, dim_scores=dim_scores)
   204→    if not subjective_entries:
   205→        return []
   206→    unassessed_dims = {
   207→        str(name).strip()
   208→        for name in unassessed_subjective_dimensions(
   209→            dim_scores
   210→        )
   211→    }
   212→
   213→    # Review findings are keyed by raw dimension name (snake_case).
   214→    review_open_by_dim: dict[str, int] = {}
   215→    for finding in findings.values():
   216→        if finding.get("status") != "open" or finding.get("detector") != "review":
   217→            continue
   218→        dim_key = str(detail_dict(finding).get("dimension", "")).strip().lower()
   219→        if not dim_key:
   220→            continue
   221→        review_open_by_dim[dim_key] = review_open_by_dim.get(dim_key, 0) + 1
   222→
   223→    items: list[dict] = []
   224→    for entry in subjective_entries:
   225→        name = str(entry.get("name", "")).strip()
   226→        if not name:
   227→            continue
   228→        strict_val = float(entry.get("strict", entry.get("score", 100.0)))
   229→        if strict_val >= threshold:
   230→            continue
   231→
   232→        [REDACTED](name)
   233→        aliases = set(_subjective_dimension_aliases(name))
   234→        cli_keys = [
   235→            str(key).strip().lower()
   236→            for key in entry.get("cli_keys", [])
   237→            if str(key).strip()
   238→        ]
   239→        aliases.update(cli_keys)
   240→        aliases.update(slugify(key) for key in cli_keys)
   241→        open_review = sum(review_open_by_dim.get(alias, 0) for alias in aliases)
   242→        is_unassessed = bool(entry.get("placeholder")) or (
   243→            name in unassessed_dims
   244→            or (strict_val <= 0.0 and int(entry.get("issues", 0)) == 0)
   245→        )
   246→        is_stale = bool(entry.get("stale"))
   247→        if is_unassessed:
   248→            primary_command = "desloppify review --prepare"
   249→        elif is_stale:
   250→            if cli_keys:
   251→                primary_command = (
   252→                    "desloppify review --prepare --dimensions " + ",".join(cli_keys)
   253→                )
   254→            else:
   255→                primary_command = "desloppify review --prepare"
   256→        else:
   257→            if open_review > 0:
   258→                primary_command = "desloppify show review --status open"
   259→            elif cli_keys:
   260→                primary_command = (
   261→                    "desloppify review --prepare --dimensions " + ",".join(cli_keys)
   262→                )
   263→            else:
   264→                primary_command = "desloppify review --prepare"
   265→        stale_tag = " [stale — re-review]" if is_stale else ""
   266→        summary = f"Subjective dimension below target: {name} ({strict_val:.1f}%){stale_tag}"
   267→        items.append(
   268→            {
   269→                "id": f"subjective::{slugify(dim_key)}",
   270→                "detector": "subjective_assessment",
   271→                "file": ".",
   272→                "tier": 4,
   273→                "effective_tier": 4,
   274→                "confidence": "medium",
   275→                "summary": summary,
   276→                "detail": {
   277→                    "dimension_name": name,
   278→                    "dimension": dim_key,
   279→                    "issues": int(entry.get("issues", 0)),
   280→                    "strict_score": strict_val,
   281→                    "open_review_findings": open_review,
   282→                    "cli_keys": cli_keys,
   283→                },
   284→                "status": "open",
   285→                "kind": "subjective_dimension",
   286→                "primary_command": primary_command,
   287→            }
   288→        )
   289→    return items
   290→
   291→
   292→__all__ = [
   293→    "ALL_STATUSES",
   294→    "ATTEST_EXAMPLE",
   295→    "build_subjective_items",
   296→    "detail_dict",
   297→    "is_review_finding",
   298→    "is_subjective_finding",
   299→    "primary_command_for_finding",
   300→    "review_finding_weight",

1→"""Dimension and overall scoring aggregation helpers."""
     2→
     3→from __future__ import annotations
     4→
     5→from dataclasses import dataclass
     6→
     7→from desloppify.core._internal.text_utils import is_numeric
     8→from desloppify.engine._scoring.detection import detector_stats_by_mode
     9→from desloppify.engine._scoring.policy.core import (
    10→    DETECTOR_SCORING_POLICIES,
    11→    DIMENSIONS,
    12→    DIMENSIONS_BY_NAME,
    13→    FAILURE_STATUSES_BY_MODE,
    14→    MECHANICAL_DIMENSION_WEIGHTS,
    15→    MECHANICAL_WEIGHT_FRACTION,
